Microscopic theory of soft run-and-tumble particles
Rosalba Garcia-Millan, Ziluo Zhang (张子洛), Luca Cocconi, Marius Bothe, Letian Chen, Zigan Zhen, Gunnar Pruessner

Abstract
Soft, repulsive run-and-tumble particles display emergent effective interactions as they appear to stick to each other in spite of the absence of attractive forces. This effective attraction emerges at strong enough repulsion and large self-propulsion. Complementing a companion paper that characterises effective attraction between two soft run-and-tumble particles Garcia-Millan et al. 2026 , here we provide a thorough derivation of our microscopic theory, which is an exact representation of the particle dynamics. We report the systematic calculation of the effective interaction vertices iteratively, in a perturbation expansion about the interaction couplings, by adding, order by order, loop corrections. We use the effective interaction vertices to calculate the two-point correlation function, fully characterising the stationary state. Other observables, such as the structure factor, overlap probability and entropy production rate are calculated as well.



\CJKnospace

I
Introduction

Active particles are out of thermodynamic equilibrium by constantly transforming a local fuel into mechanical work.
A notable phenomenon in active particle systems is the emergence of Motility-Induced Phase Separation
(MIPS), where self-propelled particles that interact repulsively tend to form clusters, separating in space
into “dilute” and “liquid” phases

Fily and Marchetti 2012
;
Cates and Tailleur 2015
;
Digregorio et al. 2018
;
Cates and Nardini 2025
.
This
stickiness
between otherwise repulsive particles
can be understood as
the emergence of effective attraction between them

Slowman et al. 2016
;
Guillin et al. 2025
;
Das et al. 2020
;
Hahn et al. 2025a
;
Metson et al. 2023
;
Hahn et al. 2025b
.
Computer simulations show that this effect also manifests
between two soft, self-propelled particles in a one-dimensional
space.
In this paper, we provide the analytical framework of the findings in
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
.
Our results establish the analytic relation between microscopic dynamics and emergence of
effective interactions between two soft repulsive self-propelled particles in a continuous periodic domain.

The contents of this paper are organised as follows.
We define the model of
N
soft run-and-tumble particles (RTPs) on
a ring interacting via a repulsive Yukawa potential
in Sec.
II
.
Using the equation of motion, given by an overdamped Langevin equation,
we derive the Fokker-Planck equation that governs the evolution of the joint particle number density
in Sec.
III
. From the Fokker-Planck equation we derive
the Doi-Peliti
Doi 1976a
;
Doi 1976b
;
Peliti 1985
;
Cardy 2006

action functional following the framework in Ref.
Pruessner and Garcia-Millan 2025
.
The action has a Gaussian part that provides the (Dyson-summed) propagators
and a non-bilinear part that provides the perturbative couplings or bare interaction vertices.

Quantifying effective interactions in the stationary state reduces to extracting the relevant properties of
the pair-correlation function,
or its counterpart in Fourier space, which is the static structure factor
Fily and Marchetti 2012
;
Hansen and McDonald 2006
.
Effective interactions are well characterised by the lowest Fourier mode of the structure factor,
which is, in turn, intimately related to the compressibility and the mean-square distance

Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
;
Hansen and McDonald 2006
.
Characterising the stationary two-point correlation function analytically requires the
renormalisation of the bare interaction vertices, which we calculate in a perturbation expansion about
the interaction coupling. Each order in the expansion is given by a new loop correction
to the two-point vertices that accounts for self-propulsion, tumbling, diffusion and bare interaction.
The sum of all terms in the perturbation expansion gives the effective interaction vertices,
which are the key mathematical object needed to calculate the stationary two-point correlation functions.
In Sec.
IV
, we devise an iterative method to calculate the effective interaction vertices order
by order in the interaction coupling analytically.

In Sec.
V
we apply the effective interaction vertices to calculate the following observables:
the static structure factor, two-point correlation functions, the overlap probability
and the entropy production rate.
These observables are complementary to the compressibility discussed in
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
,
consistently reporting on the emergence of effective attraction between active particles.
We conclude in Sec.
VI
with a discussion and an outlook.

II
Soft run-and-tumble particles

We consider the model studied in
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
of
N
interacting RTPs on a periodic one-dimensional domain
at positions
x_{i}\in[-L/2,L/2)
.
The coupled overdamped Langevin equations of motion are

\displaystyle\dot{x}_{i}=\mathsf{w}\sigma_{i}(t)-\sum_{j=1}^{N}W^{\prime}(x_{i}-x_{j})+\sqrt{2\mathsf{D}_{x}}\eta_{i}(t)\ ,

(1)

where
\mathsf{w}
is the in-built particle self-propulsion velocity,
the orientation
\sigma_{i}
is a telegraphic noise that switches
between values
\pm 1
with Poissonian rate
\gamma
,

\eta_{i}
is a thermal noise modelled by a
unit Gaussian white noise with
\mathchoice{\left\langle\eta_{i}(t)\right\rangle}{\langle\eta_{i}(t)\rangle}{\langle\eta_{i}(t)\rangle}{\langle\eta_{i}(t)\rangle}=0
and

\mathchoice{\left\langle\eta_{i}(t)\eta_{j}(t)\right\rangle}{\langle\eta_{i}(t)\eta_{j}(t)\rangle}{\langle\eta_{i}(t)\eta_{j}(t)\rangle}{\langle\eta_{i}(t)\eta_{j}(t)\rangle}=\delta_{i,j}\delta(t-t^{\prime})
and

\mathsf{D}_{x}
is the diffusion constant.
We model
soft
particle repulsion at distance
x=|x_{i}-x_{j}|

with interaction forces derived from a Yukawa potential,

\Upsilon(x)=\frac{\nu}{2\xi}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-|x|/\xi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-|x|/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-|x|/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-|x|/\xi\right)}

(2)

with interaction length
\xi
and coupling
\nu
.
Since particles move in a bounded periodic domain,
the total interaction force experienced by a particle is the sum
of forces at distance
x

modulo
the system size
L
.
The Yukawa potential on a periodic domain
x\in[-L/2,L/2)
is then

\displaystyle W(x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}\Upsilon(x+mL)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\nu\cosh((|x|-L/2)/\xi)}{2\xi\sinh(L/(2\xi))}\ ,

(3)

which approaches
\Upsilon(x)
as
\xi\ll L
.
Since interaction forces are finite for all
x
, the two particles can overlap and overcome the potential barrier at
x=0
.
We recover excluded-volume interactions in the limit
\xi\to 0
, though this scenario is not
considered in the present work.

III
Doi-Peliti action functional

The stochastic process governed by Eq. (
1
) is equivalently described by the Fokker-Planck equation for the

joint particle number density
of the vector of positions
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
and internal states

\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{N})

Zhang and Pruessner 2022
;
Pruessner and Garcia-Millan 2025
;
Zhang and Garcia-Millan 2023a
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho(\mathbf{x};\bm{\sigma};t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big\{\left(\mathsf{D}_{x}\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}-\mathsf{w}\sigma_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}-2\gamma\right)\rho(\mathbf{x};\bm{\sigma};t)

\displaystyle+{\gamma}\sum_{\sigma_{i}}\rho(\mathbf{x};\bm{\sigma};t)

\displaystyle+\partial_{x_{i}}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{j=1}\\
{j\neq i}\end{subarray}}^{N}(\partial_{x_{i}}W(x_{i}-x_{j}))\rho(\mathbf{x};\bm{\sigma};t)\Big)\Big\}\ .

(4)

The Doi-Peliti action functional
\mathcal{A}
follows immediately from the Fokker-Planck Eq. (
III
)

Pruessner and Garcia-Millan 2025
.
The action does not contain more or less information than what is provided by the Fokker-Planck equation
and the field theory is exact in the sense that it does not entail any coarse graining or approximation.
Observables are normally expressed in an order-by-order expansion of the non-linear couplings using the
language of diagrams and without losing the notion of particulate degrees of freedom
Bothe et al. 2023
.
Since the internal state of each particle is discrete, we consider for each state different species of the field:
we use the fields
\phi
,
\widetilde{\phi}
for right-moving particles (
\sigma_{i}=1
), and
\psi
,
\widetilde{\psi}
for left-moving particles (
\sigma_{i}=-1
), where
\phi
and
\psi
are annihilation fields, and
\widetilde{\phi}
and
\widetilde{\psi}
are Doi-shifted creation fields
Zhang and Pruessner 2022
;
Garcia-Millan and Pruessner 2021
.
Separating the free particle dynamics from particle interactions, the Doi-Peliti action functional
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}+\mathcal{A}_{1}
can be split into two parts
Pruessner and Garcia-Millan 2025
:
the Gaussian part (or bilinear part) of the action,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}=-\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\{

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(\partial_{t}+\mathsf{w}\partial_{x}-\mathsf{D}_{x}\partial_{x}^{2})\phi

\displaystyle+\widetilde{\psi}(\partial_{t}-\mathsf{w}\partial_{x}-\mathsf{D}_{x}\partial_{x}^{2})\psi

\displaystyle+\gamma(\tilde{\phi}-\tilde{\psi})(\phi-\psi)\}

(5)

and the perturbative part of the action,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}=-

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}{\!\mathrm{d}y\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\left(\left(1+\widetilde{\phi}(y,t)\right)\phi(y,t)+\left(1+\widetilde{\psi}(y,t)\right)\psi(y,t)\right)\partial_{x}W(x-y)\left(\phi(x,t)\partial_{x}\widetilde{\phi}(x,t)+\psi(x,t)\partial_{x}\widetilde{\psi}(x,t)\right)\ .

(6)

In this theory, the action
does not depend on the number
N
of particles in the system.
Instead, the number of particles is fixed by the initialisation at a later stage in the derivation.
We adopt the following sign convention for the action in the path integral
when calculating an observable
\mathcal{O}
,

\mathchoice{\left\langle\mathcal{O}\right\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle}=\int\mathcal{D}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\mathcal{O}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathcal{A}[\phi,\widetilde{\phi},\psi,\widetilde{\psi}]\right)}\ .

(7)

III.1
Fourier convention

We use the following Fourier transforms

\displaystyle\phi(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}\phi_{j}(\omega)\ ,

(8a)

\displaystyle\phi_{j}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}\phi(x,t)\ ,

(8b)

and correspondingly for
\widetilde{\phi}
,
\psi
and
\widetilde{\psi}
,
with wavenumber
k_{j}=2\pi j/L
and frequency
\omega
.
These transforms obey the orthogonality relations

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}{\!\mathrm{d}t\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}=

\displaystyle\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega)\ ,

(9a)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}\omega t}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega t\right)}=

\displaystyle\delta(t)\ ,

(9b)

\displaystyle\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}=

\displaystyle L\delta_{j,0}\ ,

(9c)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}\delta(x+mL)\ ,

(9d)

where we have used the notation
\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22{\omega}=\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\omega\,}/(2\pi)
,
the Dirac
\delta
-function
\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega)=2\pi\delta(\omega)
,
and the Kronecker
\delta
-function
\delta_{j,0}
.
For easier book keeping, we recognise that any Kronecker
\delta
-function
that arises from orthogonality is preceded by a factor
L
, Eq. (
9c
),
and
sums over Fourier modes
are preceded by a factor
1/L
, Eq. (
9d
), dimensionally consistent with Fourier integrals.
The (bare) interaction potential in Eq. (
3
) has the convenient Fourier transform

W_{j}=\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}W(x)=\frac{\nu\xi^{-2}}{k_{j}^{2}+\xi^{-2}}\ .

(10)

III.2
Propagators and interaction vertices

The propagators and interaction vertices are obtained by
transforming the action functional
\mathcal{A}
to Fourier space.
To ease notation we introduce the shorthands

\displaystyle G(k,\omega)=

\displaystyle-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{x}k^{2}-\mathring{\imath}\mathsf{w}k+\gamma

(11a)

\displaystyle E(k,\omega)=

\displaystyle-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathsf{D}_{x}k^{2}+\mathring{\imath}\mathsf{w}k+\gamma

(11b)

\displaystyle H=

\displaystyle\gamma

(11c)

\displaystyle D(k,\omega)=

\displaystyle(GE-H^{2})^{-1}\ ,

(11d)

which allows us to write the bare propagators of the harmonic part of the action as

\displaystyle\hbox to29.25pt{\vbox to0.8pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\displaystyle\langle{\phi_{j}(\omega)\widetilde{\phi}_{j^{\prime}}(\omega^{\prime})}\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L\delta_{j+j^{\prime},0}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\omega^{\prime})\ D(k_{j},\omega)G(k_{j},\omega)

(12a)

(12b)

\displaystyle W_{j_{3}+j_{4}}k_{j_{1}}k_{j_{3}+j_{4}}

(13d)

These four interaction vertices originate in the four combinations of forces between two species of particles:
(
13
) captures the force exerted on a right-moving particle by another right-moving particle;
(
13
), the force by a left-moving particle on a left-moving particle;
(
13
), the force by a left-moving particle on a right-moving particle; and
(
13
), the force by a right-moving particle on a left-moving particle.

IV
Effective interaction vertices

The main observable considered in the present work is the stationary two-point correlation function.
Calculating it analytically requires a renormalisation of the interaction vertices, incorporating
all loop corrections to the four-point interaction vertices in (
13
).
We study the two-particle case
N=2
,
where the derivation does not involve three-point interaction vertices
Zhang and Garcia-Millan 2023a
,
leaving the many-particle case for future work.

We therefore formally initialise the present system with two creator fields at time
t_{0}
at
x_{01}
and
x_{02}
, so that any observable
\mathcal{O}
is preceded by, say
\phi^{\dagger}(x_{02},t_{0})\phi^{\dagger}(x_{01},t_{0})
. In the case of the two-point correlations studied in the following, the only non-vanishing contributions are due to the Doi-shifted fields
\widetilde{\phi}(x_{02},t_{0})\widetilde{\phi}(x_{01},t_{0})
. To study the steady state, this initialisation ought to take place at
t_{0}\to-\infty
. Taking this limit by Fourier-transforming observables of the form
\mathchoice{\left\langle\mathcal{O}\widetilde{\phi}_{j_{02}}(\omega_{02})\widetilde{\phi}_{j_{01}(\omega_{01})}\right\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{O}\widetilde{\phi}_{j_{02}}(\omega_{02})\widetilde{\phi}_{j_{01}(\omega_{01})}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{O}\widetilde{\phi}_{j_{02}}(\omega_{02})\widetilde{\phi}_{j_{01}(\omega_{01})}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{O}\widetilde{\phi}_{j_{02}}(\omega_{02})\widetilde{\phi}_{j_{01}(\omega_{01})}\rangle}
or by using the final value theorem (or terminal-value theorem)
Schiff 1999
reveals that the limit solely affects the incoming legs, effectively reducing each such leg to a uniform
1/(2L)
from the joint
probability of the particle position and internal state at stationarity,

\displaystyle\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\langle\phi(x,t)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},t_{0})\rangle=\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\langle\psi(x,t)\widetilde{\psi}(x_{0},t_{0})\rangle=

\displaystyle=\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\langle\phi(x,t)\widetilde{\psi}(x_{0},t_{0})\rangle=\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\langle\psi(x,t)\widetilde{\phi}(x_{0},t_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2L}\ .

(14)

In Eq. (
IV
) this results in a factor
1/(2L)^{2}

from incoming legs times a symmetry factor of
2

for the two possible arrangements of incoming legs.
The uniform distribution in realspace transforms into a
\delta
-function in Fourier space, producing

L\delta_{j_{2},0}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega_{2})
and

L\delta_{j_{4},0}\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega_{4})

for each incoming leg respectively, fixing the incoming wavenumber and frequency to zero
in the vertices (
13
) and leaving an overall prefactor of
1/2
.
Diagrammatically, the initialisation in the far past,
t_{0}\to-\infty
, thus amounts to replacing the incoming legs
by a constant pre-factor
1/(2L^{2})
and a removal of these legs in their entirety

Zhen and Pruessner 2022
;
Zhang and Pruessner 2022
;
Zhang and Garcia-Millan 2023b
,
beyond a common amputation
Le Bellac 1991
.

We define the following effective interaction vertices,
which include all contributions from diagrams with no incoming legs and matching amputated outgoing legs,

(15b)

These four effective vertices encapsulate the aggregate effect of particle interactions and can
be used to calculate observables in the stationary state.

A perturbation expansion in the repulsion
\nu
shows that the correction terms of each
effective interaction vertex at order
\nu^{n}
are interdependent.
For instance, to one loop the contributions to
\Phi
are

(16)

For easier accounting we write the perturbative expansion of
\Phi
in
\nu
as

\Phi=\sum_{n\geq 1}\Phi_{n}

(17)

with
\Phi_{n}\propto\nu^{n}
.
Similarly, we define the
n
-th order in
\nu
for the other three effective vertices,

\Psi_{n}
,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}
.

Using the explicit form of the bare interaction potential (
10
) in Eq. (
13
)
with incoming legs in the steady state, Eq. (
14
), and amputated outgoing legs, the
first-order term of the effective interaction vertices are all identical

(18d)

which are defined to not contain the
L\delta
and
\delta\mkern-8.0mu\mathchar 22
functions due to translational invariance
in space and time.
The origin of
the overall factor of
1/(2L^{2})
in Eq. (
18
) is discussed after Eq. (
14
).

IV.1
Iteration

Calculating the second-order corrections
\Phi_{2}
,
\Psi_{2}
,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}_{2}

is a matter of summing over the loop corrections generated by attaching
the vertex with the correct (amputated) outgoing legs in Eqs. (
13
)
to each first-order correction in Eqs. (
18
) in every orientation.
This iteration, which increases the number of terms by a factor 8, generalises to any order
n
.
For instance, the correction
\Phi_{n+1}
is generated from

\Phi_{n}
,
\Psi_{n}
,
\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}
diagrammatically as follows,

(19)

Using the notation
G=G(k_{i},\omega)
and
G^{\prime}=G(-k_{i},-\omega)
, etc.,
for the terms defined in Eqs. (
11
)
and, similarly
\Phi=\Phi(k_{i})
and
\Phi^{\prime}=\Phi(-k_{i})
for the effective interaction vertices,
the iterative step to generate the
n+1
-th order is,

\displaystyle\Phi_{n+1}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\frac{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}DD^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times\Big\{GG^{\prime}(\Phi_{n}+\Phi_{n}^{\prime})+HG^{\prime}(\Psi_{n}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle+GH(\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}+\Psi_{n}^{\prime})+HH(\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}^{\prime})\Big\}

(20a)

\displaystyle\Psi_{n+1}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\frac{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}DD^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times\Big\{HG^{\prime}(\Phi_{n}+\Phi_{n}^{\prime})+EG^{\prime}(\Psi_{n}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle+HH(\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}+\Psi_{n}^{\prime})+EH(\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}^{\prime})\Big\}

(20b)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}_{n+1}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\frac{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}DD^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times\Big\{GH(\Phi_{n}+\Phi_{n}^{\prime})+HH(\Psi_{n}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle+GE^{\prime}(\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}+\Psi_{n}^{\prime})+HE^{\prime}(\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}^{\prime})\Big\}

(20c)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{n+1}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\frac{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}DD^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times\Big\{HH(\Phi_{n}+\Phi_{n}^{\prime})+EH(\Psi_{n}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle+HE^{\prime}(\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}+\Psi_{n}^{\prime})+EE^{\prime}(\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}^{\prime})\Big\}\ .

(20d)

The zero-mode
k_{i}=0
needs to be removed from the summations in Eqs. (
20
),
so as to correct for an artefact that arises
without a mass
r
in the propagator, normally used to
regularise the infrared.
With a positive mass, it is clear that terms in the sum of the kind
k_{i}^{2}/(\mathsf{D}_{x}k_{i}^{2}+r)
vanish at
i=0
, but this
is ambiguous if
r=0
as in
D
, Eq. (
11d
).
This caveat is further discussed in Refs.
Zhang et al. 2024
;
Zhang and Garcia-Millan 2023a

in the context of the barometric formula.
The diagrammatic representation in Eq. (
IV.1
)
corresponds to Eq. (
20
), and the diagrammatic representation of
Eqs. (
20
),
(
20
)
and (
20
)
are shown in
Eqs. (
61
) in App.
A
.

At the heart of the present work is a systematic way to calculate Eqs. (
20
).
The details of the technical steps to do this are laid out in the following subsections.

IV.2
Reparametrisation

The first step towards calculating the effective interaction vertices Eqs. (
15
) is identifying symmetries
between them that derive from symmetries in the bare propagators in Eqs. (
12
)
and the interaction vertices in Eqs. (
13
).
In App.
B
, we demonstrate that the effective vertices satisfy

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(k_{j})\ ,

(21a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}(-k_{j})\ ,

(21b)

for every
n
,
reducing the total number of effective vertices from four in Eqs. (
15
) to two.
In App.
B
, we also show that both
\Phi(x)
and
\Psi(x)
are real, and that

\Phi(x)
is even in
x
.

The integral over
\omega
in Eqs. (
20
), Sec.
IV.3
, will generally generate powers
of
k_{j}
in the denominator which are important to understand as
|k_{1}|=2\pi/L
, the smallest
|k_{j}|
in the
summations of Eqs. (
20
), is arbitrarily small in large
L
.
It turns out that these negative powers of
k_{j}
cancel neatly with the leading orders of

\Phi_{n}
and
\Psi_{n}
, which we reparametrise as

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle k_{j}^{2}P_{n}(k_{j})

(22a)

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle k_{j}^{2}Q_{n}(k_{j})+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}R_{n}(k_{j})\ ,

(22b)

with the perspective that the real functions
P(k_{j})
,
Q(k_{j})
and
R(k_{j})
are regular in small
k_{j}
, to be
confirmed below, as well as even.
The latter readily follows from physical reasoning: (Effective) potentials in real-space must be real, so that

\Phi^{*}(k_{j})=\Phi(-k_{j})

and
\Psi^{*}(k_{j})=\Psi(-k_{j})
.
The equal-species potential
\Phi(x)
must be even (and real), so that its Fourier coefficients
\Phi(k_{j})

are also real and even, while
\Psi(k_{j})
is expected to have an imaginary and odd contribution
for any finite self-propulsion.
It follows that
P
is real and even, that
Q
and
R
are even, given they are real, and further that the reparametrisation
Eqs. (
22
) does not pose a constraint on
\Phi_{n}
and
\Psi_{n}
. Instead of relying on the physical
properties of
\Phi
and
\Psi
, in the following we rederive these properties under the assumption of real

P_{n}
,
Q_{n}
,
R_{n}
on the basis of symmetries of
\Phi_{n}
and
\Psi_{n}
,
Eqs. (
66
), (
69
) and (
74
),
which we derive by induction in App.
B
.
Once
P_{n}
,
Q_{n}
,
R_{n}
are established as even, real and well behaved in small
k
, they can themselves be
parametrised more efficiently, Eq. (
37
).

Since
\Phi_{n}
is even and real, Eqs. (
66
) and (
69
),
it follows immediately that
P_{n}
is even and real.
From Eq. (
74a
), we have that

\Psi_{n}(k_{j})+\Psi^{*}_{n}(k_{j})=\Psi_{n}(k_{j})+\Psi_{n}(-k_{j})\ ,

(23)

so that the real part of
\Psi_{n}
, namely
\realpart(\Psi_{n})=(\Psi_{n}+\Psi_{n}^{*})/2
, is even in
k_{j}
and, therefore,
Q_{n}
is even and real.
Similarly, we have

\Psi_{n}(k_{j})-\Psi^{*}_{n}(k_{j})=\Psi_{n}(k_{j})-\Psi_{n}(-k_{j})\ ,

(24)

so that the imaginary part of
\Psi_{n}
, namely
\imaginarypart(\Psi_{n})=(\Psi_{n}-\Psi_{n}^{*})/2
,
is odd in
k_{j}
,
and thus
R_{n}
is even and real.
Using (
73
), we can write
Q_{n}
and
R_{n}
as

\displaystyle 2Q_{n}k_{j}^{2}=

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}\ ,

(25a)

\displaystyle 2R_{n}\mathring{\imath}k_{j}=

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}-\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}\ .

(25b)

Using the following relations derived from Eqs. (
21
), (
22
),
(
64
)
and (
71
)

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}+\Phi_{n}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{n}^{\prime}=2\Phi_{n}=2k_{j}^{2}P_{n}

(26a)

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle 2\Psi_{n}=2(k_{j}^{2}Q_{n}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}R_{n})

(26b)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}+\Psi_{n}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle 2\widetilde{\Psi}_{n}=2(k_{j}^{2}Q_{n}-\mathring{\imath}k_{j}R_{n})

(26c)

in Eq. (
20
), we obtain the recurrence relation for
P
,
Q
and
R
,

\displaystyle P_{n+1}(k_{j})k_{j}^{2}=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\frac{{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}DD^{\prime}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \times\Big\{&\phantom{+\ }2GG^{\prime}k_{i}^{2}P_{n}(k_{i})\\
&+2HG^{\prime}(k_{i}^{2}Q_{n}(k_{i})+\mathring{\imath}k_{i}R_{n}(k_{i}))\\
&+2GH(k_{i}^{2}Q_{n}(k_{i})-\mathring{\imath}k_{i}R_{n}(k_{i}))\\
&+2HHk_{i}^{2}P_{n}(k_{i})\Big\}\end{aligned}

(27a)

\displaystyle 2Q_{n+1}(k_{j})k_{j}^{2}=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\frac{{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}DD^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,\begin{aligned} \times\Big\{&\phantom{+\ }2(HG^{\prime}+GH)k_{i}^{2}P_{n}(k_{i})\\
&+2(EG^{\prime}+HH)(k_{i}^{2}Q_{n}(k_{i})+\mathring{\imath}k_{i}R_{n}(k_{i}))\\
&+2(HH+GE^{\prime})(k_{i}^{2}Q_{n}(k_{i})-\mathring{\imath}k_{i}R_{n}(k_{i}))\\
&+2(EH+HE^{\prime})k_{i}^{2}P_{n}(k_{i})\Big\}\end{aligned}

(27b)

\displaystyle 2R_{n+1}(k_{j})\mathring{\imath}k_{j}=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega^{\prime}\,}\frac{{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}DD^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,\begin{aligned} \times\Big\{&\phantom{+\ }2(HG^{\prime}-GH)k_{i}^{2}P_{n}(k_{i})\\
&+2(EG^{\prime}-HH)(k_{i}^{2}Q_{n}(k_{i})+\mathring{\imath}k_{i}R_{n}(k_{i}))\\
&+2(HH-GE^{\prime})(k_{i}^{2}Q_{n}(k_{i})-\mathring{\imath}k_{i}R_{n}(k_{i}))\\
&+2(EH-HE^{\prime})k_{i}^{2}P_{n}(k_{i})\Big\}\ .\end{aligned}

(27c)

The remaining challenge is to show that
P
,
Q
and
R
are regular in small
k_{j}
.
This amounts to showing that sum and integral in Eqs. (
27a
) and (
27
)
vanish at least linearly in
k_{j}
and that they are regular in small
k_{j}
for Eq. (
27
).
We will show this by explicitly evaluating the right-hand sides of Eqs. (
27
).

IV.3
Loop integral over
\omega

The iterative step in Eq. (
27
) involves an integral over
\omega

that can be written as linear combinations of

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}=

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}DD^{\prime}\ ,

(28a)

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}=

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}DD^{\prime}\omega\ ,

(28b)

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}=

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}DD^{\prime}\omega^{2}\ .

(28c)

As
DD^{\prime}
is even in
\omega
, Eqs. (
11
),
then the integrand of (
28b
) is odd in
\omega
and
it follows immediately that
\Omega_{1}=0
.
The four simple poles of
DD^{\prime}
, namely

\pm\mathring{\imath}a_{1}
and
\pm\mathring{\imath}a_{2}
with

a_{1}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad a_{2}=\sqrt{2\frac{\gamma}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}+\frac{\mathsf{w}^{2}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}^{2}}}

(29)

determine the integrals Eqs. (
28
) producing

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\mathsf{D}_{x}^{3}k_{j}^{2}}\frac{1}{a_{2}^{2}-a_{1}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}-\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right)\ ,

(30a)

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\mathsf{D}_{x}}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}\ .

(30b)

The value of
\Omega_{0}
is well defined because
the term
a_{2}^{2}-a_{1}^{2}={\gamma}/{\mathsf{D}_{x}}+{\mathsf{w}^{2}}/{\mathsf{D}_{x}^{2}}>0
for any
\gamma/\mathsf{D}_{x}>0
or

\mathsf{w},\mathsf{D}_{x}\neq 0
.
Substituting
G,E,H,D
(
11
) and
the integrals
\Omega_{0}
,
\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}
into
Eq. (
27
)
gives

\displaystyle P_{n+1}(k_{j})k_{j}^{2}=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\Big\{\begin{aligned} &\left[\varphi_{P0}+\frac{\varphi_{P1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\varphi_{P2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]P_{n}(k_{i})\\
+&\left[\varphi_{Q0}+\frac{\varphi_{Q1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\varphi_{Q2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]Q_{n}(k_{i})\\
+&\left[\varphi_{R0}+\frac{\varphi_{R1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\varphi_{R2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]R_{n}(k_{i})\Big\}\ ,\end{aligned}

(31)

with coefficients derived in Eq. (
75
),

\displaystyle\varphi_{P0}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}\ ,

\displaystyle\varphi_{Q0}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

\displaystyle\varphi_{R0}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

(32a)

\displaystyle\varphi_{P1}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\gamma\mathsf{w}^{2}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}^{2}}\frac{1}{\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\varphi_{Q1}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

\displaystyle\varphi_{R1}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathsf{w}\gamma}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}\frac{1}{\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2}}\ ,

(32b)

\displaystyle\varphi_{P2}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\varphi_{Q2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{\mathsf{D}_{x}^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\varphi_{R2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{w}\gamma}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}\frac{1}{\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2}}\ .

(32c)

Similarly,

\displaystyle Q_{n+1}(k_{j})k_{j}^{2}=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\Big\{\begin{aligned} &\left[\gamma_{P0}+\frac{\gamma_{P1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{P2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]P_{n}(k_{i})\\
+&\left[\gamma_{Q0}+\frac{\gamma_{Q1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{Q2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]Q_{n}(k_{i})\\
+&\left[\gamma_{R0}+\frac{\gamma_{R1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{R2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]R_{n}(k_{i})\Big\}\end{aligned}

(33)

with coefficients derived in Eq. (
76
)

\displaystyle\gamma_{P0}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

\displaystyle\gamma_{Q0}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}\ ,

\displaystyle\gamma_{R0}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

(34a)

\displaystyle\gamma_{P1}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

\displaystyle\gamma_{Q1}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

\displaystyle\gamma_{R1}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

(34b)

\displaystyle\gamma_{P2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{\mathsf{D}_{x}^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\gamma_{Q2}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}^{3}}\ ,

\displaystyle\gamma_{R2}=

\displaystyle\frac{-\mathsf{w}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}^{2}}\ ,

(34c)

and

\displaystyle R_{n+1}(k_{j})\mathring{\imath}k_{j}=

\displaystyle(-k_{j})\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{{\nu\xi^{-2}(k_{j}-k_{i})}}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\mathring{\imath}k_{i}\Big\{\begin{aligned} &\left[\eta_{P0}+\frac{\eta_{P1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\eta_{P2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]P_{n}(k_{i})\\
+&\left[\eta_{Q0}+\frac{\eta_{Q1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\eta_{Q2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]Q_{n}(k_{i})\\
+&\left[\eta_{R0}+\frac{\eta_{R1}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\eta_{R2}}{k_{i}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right]R_{n}(k_{i})\Big\}\ ,\end{aligned}

(35)

with coefficients derived in Eq. (
77
)

\displaystyle\eta_{P0}=0\ ,\ \eta_{Q0}=0\ ,\ \eta_{R0}=0\ ,

(36a)

\displaystyle\eta_{P1}=\frac{\gamma\mathsf{w}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}(\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2})}\ ,\ \eta_{Q1}=0\ ,\ \eta_{R1}=\frac{\gamma}{\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2}}\ ,

(36b)

\displaystyle\eta_{P2}=-\frac{\gamma\mathsf{w}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}(\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2})}\ ,\ \eta_{Q2}=\frac{\mathsf{w}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}^{2}}\ ,\ \eta_{R2}=\frac{\mathsf{w}^{2}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}(\gamma\mathsf{D}_{x}+\mathsf{w}^{2})}\ .

(36c)

To proceed further, we introduce a reparameterisation of the even functions
P
,
Q
and
R
,

\displaystyle P_{n}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle{(\nu\xi^{-2})^{n}}\sum_{m=1}^{M_{n}}\pi_{n,m}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+p^{2}_{n,m}}\ ,

(37a)

\displaystyle Q_{n}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle{(\nu\xi^{-2})^{n}}\sum_{m=1}^{M_{n}}\zeta_{n,m}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+p^{2}_{n,m}}\ ,

(37b)

\displaystyle R_{n}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle{(\nu\xi^{-2})^{n}}\sum_{m=1}^{M_{n}}\rho_{n,m}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+p^{2}_{n,m}}\ ,

(37c)

with simple poles at
\pm\mathring{\imath}p_{n,m}
.
The number of those poles at any order
n
of the iterative scheme Eqs. (
IV.3
),
(
IV.3
) and (
IV.3
)
as well as their values are determined below.
The parametrisation in Eq. (
37
) implicitly assumes that
P
,
Q
and
R

have simple poles only. As it is shown below, this is not the case because double and higher-order
poles are generated under the iteration. However, we show below that the amplitude multiplying them is
exponentially,
\propto\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L/\xi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}
, small.
From Eqs. (
18
) and (
22
), we have

\displaystyle P_{1}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\nu\xi^{-2}}}{2L^{2}}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+\xi^{-2}}

(38a)

\displaystyle Q_{1}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\nu\xi^{-2}}}{2L^{2}}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+\xi^{-2}}

(38b)

\displaystyle R_{1}(k_{j})=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

(38c)

so that

p_{1,1}=\xi^{-1}\ ,

(39)

and

\displaystyle\pi_{1,1}=-\frac{1}{2L^{2}},\

\displaystyle\zeta_{1,1}=-\frac{1}{2L^{2}},\

\displaystyle\rho_{1,1}=0\ .

(40)

In the following, we adopt the language of “pole
p_{n,m}
” and “contributions to pole
p_{n,m}
”
when we mean poles at
\pm\mathring{\imath}p_{n,m}
and contributions to
\hat{A}
in a term of the form

\hat{A}/(k_{j}^{2}+p^{2}_{n,m})
respectively, which has residues
\pm\hat{A}/(2\mathring{\imath}p_{n,m})
.
The “amplitude”
\hat{A}
is
\pi_{n,m}
,
\zeta_{n,m}
and
\rho_{n,m}
respectively in Eqs. (
37
).
The iterative scheme Eqs. (
IV.3
), (
IV.3
) and (
IV.3
)
with coefficients in Eqs. (
32
), (
34
) and (
36
) can be found explicitly, in dimensionless
form, in App.
G
.

IV.4
Loop sum over
k_{j}

Substituting
P_{n}
,
Q_{n}
,
R_{n}
in Eqs. (
37
) into the iterative scheme in
Eqs. (
IV.3
), (
IV.3
) and (
IV.3
)
gives the loop sums in
k_{j}
that are needed to derive the
(n+1)
-th order.
These sums are generally convolutions, with one term of the form
((k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2})^{-1}
multiplying terms of the form
(k_{i}^{2}+\alpha)^{-1}
, either in the square brackets of Eqs. (
IV.3
), (
IV.3
) and (
IV.3
) or in the
P_{n}
,
Q_{n}
,
R_{n}
, Eqs. (
37
), multiplying them. Specifically, the coefficients with index
0
, such as
\varphi_{P0}
,
\varphi_{Q0}
and
\varphi_{R0}
in Eq. (
IV.3
), and similarly
\gamma_{P0}

etc.
in Eq. (
IV.3
), multiply only the fractions
(k_{i}^{2}+\alpha)^{-1}
in Eq. (
37
), whereas the coefficients
\varphi_{P1}
,
\varphi_{Q1}
,
\varphi_{R1}
and
\varphi_{P2}

etc.
multiply products of the form
(k_{i}^{2}+\alpha)^{-1}(k_{i}^{2}+\beta)^{-1}
, with one term explicitly in the square brackets and one in the
P_{n}
,
Q_{n}
,
R_{n}
, Eqs. (
37
). To bring these products
(k_{i}^{2}+\alpha)^{-1}(k_{i}^{2}+\beta)^{-1}
into the form of sums of
(k_{i}^{2}+\alpha)^{-1}
and
(k_{i}^{2}+\beta)^{-1}
with suitable amplitudes, we write them for
\alpha\neq\beta
as partial fractions,

\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+\alpha^{2}}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+\beta^{2}}=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+\beta^{2}}-\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+\alpha^{2}}\right)\ .

(41)

This reduces all sums to
the following loop sums over
k_{i}
, which are known as Matsubara sums

Matsubara 1955
;
Espinosa 2010
,
and are
derived in App.
D
by applying the summation theorem

Spiegel et al. 2009
;
Marsden and Hoffman 1973
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\frac{k_{j}-k_{i}}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}\frac{1}{k_{i}^{2}+\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k_{j}}{2\alpha}\left(\frac{A(\alpha)}{k_{j}^{2}+(\alpha+\xi^{-1})^{2}}+\frac{B(\alpha)}{k_{j}^{2}+(\alpha-\xi^{-1})^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{2}{L\alpha}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+\xi^{-2}}\right)

(42a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathds{Z}\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\frac{k_{j}-k_{i}}{(k_{j}-k_{i})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}\frac{k_{i}}{k_{i}^{2}+\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}{\left(\frac{A(\alpha)(\alpha+\xi^{-1})}{k_{j}^{2}+(\alpha+\xi^{-1})^{2}}+\frac{B(\alpha)(\alpha-\xi^{-1})}{k_{j}^{2}+(\alpha-\xi^{-1})^{2}}\right)}\ ,

(42b)

with coefficients

\displaystyle A(\alpha)=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L(\alpha+\xi^{-1})}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(\alpha+\xi^{-1})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(\alpha+\xi^{-1})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(\alpha+\xi^{-1})\right)}}{(1-\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L\alpha}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)})(1-\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L/\xi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)})}\ ,

(43a)

\displaystyle B(\alpha)=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L\alpha}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}-\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L/\xi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}}{(1-\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L\alpha}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)})(1-\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L/\xi}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L/\xi\right)})}\ .

(43b)

Of these,
A(\alpha)
may be thought of as “order
1
” and
B(\alpha)
may be thought of as vanishing, because for
L\alpha
large, as we will assume, and
L\xi^{-1}
large anyway,
the amplitudes are
A(\alpha)=1+\mathcal{O}(\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L\alpha}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)})
and
B(\alpha)=\mathcal{O}(\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L\alpha}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L\alpha\right)})
, Eqs. (
48
).
For
L\gg\alpha
, the exponentials essentially vanish.
Although we keep track of terms proportional to both
A(\alpha)
and
B(\alpha)

we will draw on the fact that
B(\alpha)
is small when considering some of the poles,
in particular repeated ones. These are not covered by Eq. (
41
),
as it assumes
\alpha\neq\beta
, and are difficult to consider in full generality.
The loop sum in (
42a
) features in
P
and
Q
, Eqs. (
IV.3
) and (
IV.3
),
whereas the loop sum in (
42b
)
features in
R
, Eq. (
IV.3
), as the curly bracket is multiplied by
k_{i}
.

IV.5
Iterative generation of poles

Figure 1:

Pole generation
p_{n,m}^{(i)}
order by order (left)
\xi
-type
p_{n,m}^{(0)}=m\xi^{-1}

and (right)
a_{i}
-type
p_{n,m}^{(i)}=a_{i}+m\xi^{-1}
, Eq. (
45
).
Arrows indicate up-promotion with amplitude
A
, Eq. (
43a
) (red),
and down-promotion with amplitude
B
, Eq. (
43b
) (black).
Base case
p_{1,1}^{(0)}=\xi^{-1}
, Eq. (
39
), is encircled in purple.
Poles of
\xi
-type encircled on the left
in orange are generated at every order through the final term in Eq. (
42a
)
\sim L^{-1}
,
which does not carry a factor of
A
or
B
.
The poles labelled blue on the right are populated at every order from
a_{i}
-poles in the square brackets in
Eqs. (
IV.3
), (
IV.3
) and (
IV.3
),
which via Eq. (
41
) are shifted by Eq. (
42
) to
a_{i}\pm\xi^{-1}
, so that

p_{n,-1}^{(i)}=a_{i}-\xi^{-1}
carries a factor of
B(a_{i})
and

p_{n,1}^{(i)}=a_{i}+\xi^{-1}
a factor of
A(a_{i})
.
The orange and blue labelled poles are the only ones freshly populated in the scheme, the amplitude of all others
“inherit” the amplitudes of those in previous generations.
As a result, all
a_{i}
-type poles for
m\leq 0
(faded) carry a factor of
B

and are thus omitted in the present scheme.
Double poles generated from
p_{n,0}^{(i)}=a_{i}
are also omitted.
There is no shift below
p_{n,1}^{(0)}=\xi^{-1}
on the left because of Eq. (
44
).

The loop sums in Eq. (
42
) reveal how the poles in
k_{j}
arise iteratively
as we proceed from
n
loops to
n+1
loops.
We find that every pole
\alpha
that exists to
n
loops is shifted “to the right” by
\xi^{-1}

(up-promotion)
as well as “to the left” by
\xi^{-1}
(down-promotion), generating in the next order the new poles

\alpha+\xi^{-1}
and
\alpha-\xi^{-1}
respectively.
The amplitude with which they contribute to the next order is proportional to the functions
A
and
B
respectively, showing that the “down-promotion” of poles has exponentially vanishing amplitude for large system sizes

L\gg\xi
,
a_{i}^{-1}
, as discussed in detail below.
The algebraic term in
L
in
Eq. (
42a
) further shows an additional contribution to the pole
\xi^{-1}
at every order
n
.
In this iterative generation of poles, Eq. (
42a
) seems to produce a pole at
k_{j}=0
through
B
.
However,
the special case of
\alpha=\xi^{-1}
,
which produces the term

\frac{k_{j}}{2\xi^{-1}}\frac{B(\xi^{-1})}{k_{j}^{2}}=0

(44)

is resolved by a zero amplitude

B(\xi^{-1})=0
as given by Eq. (
43b
).

At first order,
n=1
, there is actually only one pole, namely
\xi^{-1}
in (
39
).
However, for better book keeping it pays to consider two more poles,

a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, Eqs. (
29
),
even when they feature with vanishing amplitude at
n=1
.
Via Eqs. (
41
) and (
42
),
the poles enter at order
n=2
as
a_{1,2}\pm\xi^{-1}
.
We refer to these poles as different types, “
\xi
-type”, “
a_{1}
-type” and “
a_{2}
-type”,
and use a superscript for quantities related to each pole type.
The set of poles at each order
n
, generated through (
42
) is covered by

\displaystyle p^{(0)}_{n,{m}}=

\displaystyle m\xi^{-1}

\displaystyle\text{for }m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\ ,

(45a)

\displaystyle p^{(1)}_{n,m}=

\displaystyle a_{1}+m\xi^{-1}

\displaystyle\text{for }m\in\{-(n-1),\ldots,n-1\}\ ,

(45b)

\displaystyle p^{(2)}_{n,m}=

\displaystyle a_{2}+m\xi^{-1}

\displaystyle\text{for }m\in\{-(n-1),\ldots,n-1\}\ ,

(45c)

where amplitudes for
m\leq 0
of
a_{i}
-type poles in (
45b
) and (
45c
)
are treated as
0
, as discussed below.
Counting all poles, there are
M_{n}=5n-2
poles at order
n
, of which
2n
are treated as having vanishing amplitude.

Fig.
1
illustrates the mechanisms by which poles are generated iteratively.
The shift by
\pm\xi^{-1}
produces the same poles by different paths, resulting in different contributions
to its amplitude. For example, the pole
a_{1}+\xi^{-1}
is generated
by shifting
a_{1}
by
+\xi^{-1}
and by shifting
a_{1}+2\xi^{-1}
by
-\xi^{-1}
.

It is cumbersome, however, to determine the different contributions to poles in one
generation from the poles in the previous generation. For example, the pole
a_{1}+\xi^{-1}
in generation
n+1

may have contributions from
a_{1}
in the square brackets of
Eqs. (
IV.3
), (
IV.3
) and (
IV.3
)
via Eq. (
41
), but from
a_{1}+2\xi^{-1}
only if this pole exists in the
previous generation
n
,
i.e.
provided
n-1\geq 2
. Finding these conditions retrospectively
is an arduous task.
Rather than writing down an explicit sum over all contributions to a given new pole,
it is therefore more efficient to assign a given contribution to a particular residue in the next generation.
Similarly, it is not necessary to keep track of
a_{i}
-type poles that are not populated at a given generation,
it suffices to assign them a vanishing amplitude.

Neglecting double and higher-order poles simplifies the iterative calculation.
Double poles are generated amongst

a_{i}
-type poles with
m=0
at orders
n\geq 3
through
shifts of poles at
a_{i}\pm\xi^{-1}
. Those at
a_{i}-\xi^{-1}
are created with an amplitude

B(a_{i})=\mathcal{O}(\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-La_{i}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-La_{i}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-La_{i}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-La_{i}\right)})
and are thus exponentially suppressed before shifted to
a_{i}
with a factor

A(a_{i}-\xi^{-1})=1+\mathcal{O}(\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L(a_{i}-\xi^{-1})}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(a_{i}-\xi^{-1})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(a_{i}-\xi^{-1})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(a_{i}-\xi^{-1})\right)})
.
Those at
a_{i}+\xi^{-1}
are created with
A(a_{i})=1+\mathcal{O}(\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-La_{i}}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-La_{i}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-La_{i}\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-La_{i}\right)})
and subsequently multiplied by

B(a_{i}+\xi^{-1})=\mathcal{O}(\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-L(a_{i}+\xi^{-1})}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(a_{i}+\xi^{-1})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(a_{i}+\xi^{-1})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-L(a_{i}+\xi^{-1})\right)})
.
Amplitudes of repeated poles are therefore exponentially suppressed provided
L\xi^{-1}\gg 1
as well as

La_{1}\gg 1
and
La_{2}\gg 1
, Eq. (
43b
), or equally
L^{2}\gamma/\mathsf{D}_{x}\gg 1
, Eq. (
29
).
Making this simplifying assumption is a matter of pragmatism.
Sums such as Eq. (
42
) can be determined for a double pole
\alpha
, for example by differentiation
with respect to
\alpha
. Their results generate higher-order repeated poles, which are, in turn,
propagated through up/down-promotion.
To circumvent the generation of a cascade of higher-order poles, we ignore poles at

\pm\mathring{\imath}p^{(i)}_{n,0}
for
n\geq 3
.
As all
a_{i}
-type poles for
m\leq 0
have at least one exponentially small factor of
B
we ignore all of those
in our scheme, which thus requires
L/\xi\gg 1
, as well as
L^{2}\gamma/\mathsf{D}_{x}\gg 1
, Eq. (
29
).

In summary, our iterative scheme follows from substituting the loop sums in Eqs. (
42
) into
Eqs. (
IV.3
), (
IV.3
) and (
IV.3
)
and
collecting all contributions to order
n+1
from each pole at order
n
.
Instead of writing the explicit form of the effective interaction vertices

\Phi
,
\widetilde{\Phi}
,
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
(
22
),
or equivalently
P
,
Q
and
R
(
37
),
we identify the contribution from each term in
P
,
Q
and
R
at oder
n
to the coefficients

\pi_{n+1,m}
,
\zeta_{n+1,m}
and
\rho_{n+1,m}
at order
n+1
with the initial values
in Eq. (
40
) for
\xi
-type poles,
and
\pi^{(i)}_{n,m}=\zeta^{(i)}_{n,m}=\rho^{(i)}_{n,m}=0
for
a_{i}
-type poles.
The remaining algebra is detailed in Apps.
E
and

F
.

IV.6
Sums vs integrals

In the large
L
limit, the Riemann sums in Eq. (
42
) over discrete Fourier modes
k_{i}
converge to
integrals over continuous
k^{\prime}
,

\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i\in\mathds{Z}}f(k_{i})=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}f(k^{\prime})

(46)

for arbitrary
f(k)
.
The loop integrals that approximate the sums in Eq. (
42
) are

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}\frac{k-k^{\prime}}{(k-k^{\prime})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}\frac{1}{k^{\prime 2}+\alpha^{2}}=

\displaystyle\frac{k}{2\alpha}\frac{1}{k^{2}+(\alpha+\xi^{-1})^{2}}

(47a)

\displaystyle\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22k^{\prime}\,}\frac{k-k^{\prime}}{(k-k^{\prime})^{2}+\xi^{-2}}\frac{k^{\prime}}{k^{\prime 2}+\alpha^{2}}=

\displaystyle-\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{(1/2)}{\frac{1}{2}}{(1/2)}\frac{\alpha+\xi^{-1}}{k^{2}+(\alpha+\xi^{-1})^{2}}\ ,

(47b)

for real and positive
\alpha
, such as
\alpha=p^{(0)}_{1,1}=\xi^{-1}
or
\alpha=p^{(i)}_{1,0}=a_{i}
.
With continuous
k
, the generation of poles has a much simpler structure
since the only mechanism at play is the “up-promotion”
of any pole
\alpha
by
\xi^{-1}
to the right.
As poles
a_{i}
are populated at every order by Eq. (
41
), the set of poles reduces to

2n-1
, namely

p_{n}^{(0)}=n\xi^{-1}
and
p_{n}^{(i)}=a_{i}+m\xi^{-1}
for
m\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
.
In Fig.
1
, all black arrows vanish in this limit, as well as
\xi
-type poles encircled in orange and
poles generated from them.

The coefficients

A(\alpha)

and

B(\alpha)

in Eq. (
43
)
have limits

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}A(\alpha)=

\displaystyle 1\ ,

(48a)

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}B(\alpha)=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

(48b)

so that taking this limit on the right of Eq. (
42
) recovers
the loop integrals Eq. (
47
) for fixed
k_{j}=k
.
While finite-size corrections contained in
A
and
B
in Eq. (
43
) decay exponentially,
the third term in Eq. (
42a
) implements algebraic corrections in
L
that originate from
the removal of the
0
-mode.
These algebraic corrections have a noticeable effect on the particle statistics,
but are missed when simply replacing sums with integrals.

V
Observables

Having derived the effective interaction vertices
\Phi
and
\Psi

Eqs. (
15
) and
(
22
)
or, equivalently,
P
,
Q
and
R
, calculating observables in the stationary state is a matter of
attaching the suitable outgoing legs to the effective vertices.
In the following, we use the dimensionless parameters

\bar{\xi}={\xi}/{L}
,

\bar{\nu}={\nu}/{(\mathsf{D}_{x}\xi)}
,

\mathsf{Pe}={\mathsf{w}^{2}}/{(\mathsf{D}_{x}\gamma)}
, and

\bar{\gamma}={\gamma\xi^{2}}/{\mathsf{D}_{x}}
.

V.1
Structure factor

The static structure factor is defined as the Fourier transform of the pair correlation function
P(x_{1},x_{2})

Hansen and McDonald 2006

S_{i,j}=\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathring{\imath}(k_{i}x_{1}+k_{j}x_{2})}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}(k_{i}x_{1}+k_{j}x_{2})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}(k_{i}x_{1}+k_{j}x_{2})\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(-\mathring{\imath}(k_{i}x_{1}+k_{j}x_{2})\right)}P(x_{1},x_{2})\ .

(49)

The stationary pair correlation function, or two-point particle number density, reads

\displaystyle P(x_{1},x_{2})=

\displaystyle\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\big\langle(\phi(x_{1},t)+\psi(x_{1},t))

\displaystyle(\phi(x_{2},t)+\psi(x_{2},t))\phi^{\dagger}(y_{1},t_{0})\phi^{\dagger}(y_{2},t_{0})\big\rangle\ .

(50)

It is the probability density to find one particle of any species at
x_{1}
and another one at
x_{2}
,
so that its normalisation is
2
.
In steady state, it is a function only of the difference
x_{1}-x_{2}
, in fact it is even in the difference
and
L
-periodic in both arguments.
Attaching the bare propagators in Eq. (
12
) as outgoing legs to the
effective interaction vertices defined in Eq. (
15
),
the pair correlation function is

\displaystyle S_{i,j}=

\displaystyle L\delta_{i+j,0}\Big\{\frac{2}{L^{2}}L\delta_{i,0}+\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}DD^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times\Big[\begin{aligned} &(GG^{\prime}+HG^{\prime}+GH+HH)(\Phi+\Phi^{\prime})\\
+&(HG^{\prime}+EG^{\prime}+HH+EH)(\Psi+\widetilde{\Psi}^{\prime})\\
+&(GH+HH+GE^{\prime}+HE^{\prime})(\Psi^{\prime}+\widetilde{\Psi})\\
+&(HH+EH+HE^{\prime}+EE^{\prime})(\widetilde{\Phi}+\widetilde{\Phi}^{\prime})\Big]\Big\}\ .\end{aligned}

(51)

Defining
S_{j}=\sum_{i}S_{i,j}=S_{-j,j}
and
substituting Eq. (
26
) into (
V.1
) before taking the integral over
\omega

using the functions
f_{P}
,
f_{Q}
,
f_{R}
,
g_{P}
,
g_{Q}
,
g_{R}
defined in Eqs. (
75
) and (
76
),
we obtain

\displaystyle S_{j}=

\displaystyle 2\delta_{j,0}+2L\sum_{n=1}\Big[\begin{aligned} &\big(f_{P}(k_{j})+f_{Q}(k_{j})\big)P_{n}(k_{j})\\
+&\big(g_{P}(k_{j})+g_{Q}(k_{j})\big)Q_{n}(k_{j})\\
+&\big(f_{R}(k_{j})+g_{R}(k_{j})\big)R_{n}(k_{j})\Big]\ .\end{aligned}

(52)

Calculating the structure factor requires the evaluation of
P_{n}
,
Q_{n}
,
R_{n}

iteratively up to a desired order
n
in
\nu
, Eqs. (
37
).
The structure factor
S_{j}
is shown in Fig.
2
as a function of
j
for increasing activity.
In the passive case,
\mathsf{Pe}=0
, the structure factor
S_{j}
is negative for all wavelengths
k_{j}
.
As the activity
\mathsf{Pe}
increases, the lowest modes increase as well, eventually rendering
S_{j}
positive.
The lowest mode, the compressibility factor
S_{1}
, is the fingerprint of effective attraction or effective repulsion
as introduced in
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
.
For
\xi\ll L
, so that
k_{1}\xi\ll 1
, we obtain the following asymptotic behaviour in small

\bar{\xi}=\xi/L
of the compressibility
S_{1}

by direct substitution of the coefficients in Eqs. (
75
) and (
76
) in
Eq. (
52
),

\displaystyle S_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{2L}{\mathsf{D}_{x}(1+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{Pe})}\sum_{n\geq 1}\left(P_{n}(0)+Q_{n}(0)-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{Pe}}{\bar{\gamma}}}\xi R_{n}(0)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\bar{\xi}^{2}\right)\ .

(53)

This simple expression, which features
the effective diffusivity
\mathsf{D}_{\text{eff}}=\mathsf{D}_{x}(1+\mathsf{Pe}/2)
in the denominator of
S_{1}
,
captures the essence of how the effective attraction is encoded in the
perturbation expansion.
As discussed in App.
G
, the sign of
P_{n}
and
Q_{n}
alternates between positive and
negative for even and odd
n
, while
\xi R_{n}
takes small numerical values compared to
P_{n}
and
Q_{n}
.
Thus, the sign of each term in the expansion of
S_{1}
essentially
follows the sign of
P_{n}
and
Q_{n}
, while the amplitude of each term is reduced by
R_{n}

depending on
\sqrt{\mathsf{Pe}/\bar{\gamma}}
.
This crucially affects odd terms in the expansion, which are the ones that carry the sign of
effective interactions since they are positive for bare attraction and negative for bare repulsion
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
.
Thus, odd terms in the expansion of
S_{1}
are the only ones that can render
S_{1}
negative due to
bare repulsion.
The suppression of odd terms by
R_{n}
in the expansion then leads to
S_{1}>0

for large enough
\bar{\nu}
and
\mathsf{Pe}>0
.
The compressibility
S_{1}
is written explicitly up to second order in
\bar{\nu}
in App.
H
.

Figure 2:
Structure factor
S_{j}
as a function of Fourier mode
j
for varying activity
\mathsf{Pe}=\mathsf{w}^{2}/(\mathsf{D}_{x}\gamma)
.
Parameters:

\mathsf{D}_{x}=2
,

L=20
,

\bar{\nu}=5
,

\bar{\xi}=0.1
,

\bar{\gamma}=0.02
.

V.2
Two-point correlation functions

Figure 3:

Stationary two-point correlation functions
P_{++}
and
P_{-+}

Eqs. (
55
) as a function of the inter-particle distance
x_{1}-x_{2}
,
for increasing interaction coupling
\bar{\nu}
.
Parameters correspond to Fig. 2(a) in
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
:

D=0.5
,
L=20
,
\mathsf{Pe}=20
,
\bar{\gamma}=0.008
,
\bar{\xi}=0.01
.

Figure 4:

Stationary two-point correlation functions
P_{++}
and
P_{-+}

Eqs. (
55
) as a function of the inter-particle distance
x_{1}-x_{2}
,
for increasing activity
\mathsf{Pe}
.
Parameters correspond to Fig. 2(b) in
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
:

D=0.5
,
L=20
,
\bar{\nu}=10
,
\bar{\gamma}=0.008
,
\bar{\xi}=0.01
.

The two-point correlation function
P(x_{1},x_{2})
, or two-point particle number density in Eq. (
V.1
),
is calculated by inverting
the Fourier transform Eq. (
49
) of the structure factor in (
52
).
Using Eq. (
41
) and

\frac{1}{L}\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}\frac{1}{k_{j}^{2}+\alpha^{2}}=\ \frac{\cosh\left(\left(|x|-\frac{L}{2}\right)\alpha\right)}{2\alpha\sinh\left(\frac{L}{2}\alpha\right)}\ ,

(54)

the inverse Fourier transform of the structure factor
is a linear combination of (
54
) where
\alpha
takes the value of every pole
at each order in
\nu
. The inverse Fourier transform can thus be calculated exactly.
The two-point correlation function
P(x_{1},x_{2})
, which is shown in Fig. 2 of
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
,
has normalisation such that

\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}P(x_{1},x_{2})=2
.

We also derive the steady-state joint probability densities

\displaystyle P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}}}(x_{1},x_{2})=

\displaystyle\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\big\langle\phi(x_{1},t)\phi(x_{2},t)\phi^{\dagger}(y_{1},t_{0})\phi^{\dagger}(y_{2},t_{0})\big\rangle\ ,

(55a)

\displaystyle P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}}}(x_{1},x_{2})=

\displaystyle\lim_{t_{0}\to-\infty}\big\langle\psi(x_{1},t)\phi(x_{2},t)\phi^{\dagger}(y_{1},t_{0})\phi^{\dagger}(y_{2},t_{0})\big\rangle\ ,

(55b)

which amounts to a calculation similar to the effective vertices

\Phi_{n+1}
and
\Psi_{n+1}
in Eq. (
20
).
Defining
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}},i,j}=\delta_{i+j,0}
and similarly
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}},i,j}
as the Fourier transforms of Eqs. (
55
)
in
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
only, Eq. (
8b
) without the integral over
t
, these observables can be expressed
in terms of the Fourier-transformed fields, such as
\phi_{j}(\omega)
.
The steady state nature of Eqs. (
55
) then results in an integral over
\omega
. Defining further

P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}},j}=\sum_{i}P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}},i,j}=P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}},-j,j}

and
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}},j}=\sum_{i}P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}},i,j}=P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}},-j,j}
,
we have

\displaystyle P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}},j}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\delta_{j,0}+L\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}DD^{\prime}\Big[GG^{\prime}(\Phi+\Phi^{\prime})+HG^{\prime}(\Psi+\widetilde{\Psi}^{\prime})+GH(\Psi^{\prime}+\widetilde{\Psi})+HH(\widetilde{\Phi}+\widetilde{\Phi}^{\prime})\Big]

(56a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\delta_{j,0}+L\sum_{n=1}\Big[\begin{aligned} &\left(\varphi_{P0}+\frac{\varphi_{P1}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\varphi_{P2}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right)P_{n}(k_{j})\\
+&\left(\varphi_{Q0}+\frac{\varphi_{Q1}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\varphi_{Q2}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right)Q_{n}(k_{j})\\
+&\left(\varphi_{R0}+\frac{\varphi_{R1}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\varphi_{R2}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right)R_{n}(k_{j})\Big]\end{aligned}

(56b)

and

\displaystyle P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}},j}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\delta_{j,0}+L\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}{\!\mathrm{d}\mkern-7.0mu\mathchar 22\omega\,}DD^{\prime}\Big[HG^{\prime}(\Phi+\Phi^{\prime})+EG^{\prime}(\Psi+\widetilde{\Psi}^{\prime})+HH(\Psi^{\prime}+\widetilde{\Psi})+EH(\widetilde{\Phi}+\widetilde{\Phi}^{\prime})\Big]

(57a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\delta_{j,0}+L\sum_{n=1}\Big[\begin{aligned} &\left(\gamma_{P0}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{P0}+\frac{\gamma_{P1}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{P1}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{P2}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{P2}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right)P_{n}(k_{j})\\
+&\left(\gamma_{Q0}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{Q0}+\frac{\gamma_{Q1}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{Q1}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{Q2}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{Q2}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right)Q_{n}(k_{j})\\
+&\left(\gamma_{R0}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{R0}+\frac{\gamma_{R1}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{R1}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{R2}+\mathring{\imath}k_{j}\eta_{R2}}{k_{j}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}}\right)R_{n}(k_{j})\Big]\ .\end{aligned}

(57b)

Using the identities of partial fractions in Eq. (
41
)
and the periodic Yukawa potential in Eq. (
54
),
as well as the derivative of the latter with respect to
x
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\mathrm{e}^{\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x}}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}{\operatorname{exp}\left(\mathring{\imath}k_{j}x\right)}\frac{\mathring{\imath}k_{j}}{k_{j}^{2}+\alpha^{2}}=\ {\sign(x)}\frac{\sinh\left(\left(|x|-\frac{L}{2}\right)\alpha\right)}{2\sinh\left(\frac{L}{2}\alpha\right)}\ ,

(58)

the correlation functions
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}}}(x_{1},x_{2})
and
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}}}(x_{1},x_{2})
are calculated analytically in real space.
Fig.
3
shows the two-point correlation functions
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}}}
and
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}}}
for increasing interaction
coupling
\bar{\nu}
from left to right.
We find that, as
\bar{\nu}
increases, the emergence of a maximum in
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}}}
becomes more pronounced
indicating the emergence of a bound state between two RTPs with opposite orientation.
Thus, counterintuitively, a stronger repulsive force leads to effective attraction
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
.
The emergence of bound states, although weaker, is also found between RTPs with equal orientation, as
indicated by the two maxima in
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}}}
. There is no bare mechanism in the Langevin Eq. (
1
) that generates
these bound states. Their origin lies in the effective resetting at short distances that takes place through tumbling,
as one of the head-on colliding particles reorients, resulting in same-species particles
being found close to each other.
In Fig.
4
we show the same correlation functions but for increasing activity
\mathsf{Pe}
from left to right
at fixed interaction coupling
\bar{\nu}
. Driving the system away from equilibrium by activity results,
similar to Fig.
3
, in the
emergence of bound states between both RTPs independently of their relative orientations.
As
\mathsf{Pe}
increases, the position of the maximum in
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}}}
,
which defines the accumulation distance
x_{\text{A}}

Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
, decreases.
In other words, both RTPs tend to
accumulate at closer distance from each other on average, as shown in Fig. 3(b) of
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
.

V.3
Overlap probability

Figure 5:

Overlap probability
p_{\xi}
rescaled by its value in the non-interacting case,
p_{\xi}=2\xi/L
,
as a function of activity
\mathsf{Pe}
for varying tumbling rate
\bar{\gamma}
.
Symbols indicate the value of
\mathsf{Pe}
at which the compressibility factor
S_{1}

changes sign from
S_{1}<0
(small
\mathsf{Pe}
, effective repulsion) to
S_{1}>0
(large
\mathsf{Pe}
, effective attraction).
Parameters:

\mathsf{D}_{x}=1
,

L=20
,

\bar{\nu}=5
, and

\bar{\xi}=0.01
.

Having characterised the steady state analytically, we now use the correlation functions to calculate
other quantities. An observable of interest is, for instance, the overlap probability
p_{\xi}
, namely the probability
that the two particles are found at a shorter distance than
\xi
,

p_{\xi}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,}\int_{x_{1}-\xi}^{x_{1}+\xi}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,}P(x_{1},x_{2})\ .

(59)

Since both particles are soft, they overlap and even cross each other due to fluctuations.
The overlap probability
rescaled by its value in the non-interacting case
p_{\xi}=2\bar{\xi}
, where
P(x_{1},x_{2})=2/L^{2}
,
is shown in Fig.
5

as a function of activity
\mathsf{Pe}
for varying tumbling rate
\bar{\gamma}
.
We find that the overlap probability
p_{\xi}
increases with increasing activity and increasing tumbling rate.
The underlying jamming mechanism is the cause for this increase in overlap.

V.4
Entropy production rate

Figure 6:

Entropy production rate
\dot{\mathcal{S}}

rescaled by its value in the non-interacting case,
\dot{\mathcal{S}}=2\mathsf{w}^{2}/\mathsf{D}_{x}
,
as a function of interaction coupling
\bar{\nu}
for varying interaction length
\bar{\xi}
.
Symbols indicate the value of
\bar{\nu}
at which the compressibility factor
S_{1}

changes sign from
S_{1}<0
(small
\bar{\nu}
, effective repulsion) to
S_{1}>0
(large
\bar{\nu}
, effective attraction).
Parameters:

\mathsf{D}_{x}=1
,

L=20
,

\bar{\gamma}=0.05
, and

\mathsf{Pe}=10
.

Another observable of interest is the entropy production rate
\dot{\mathcal{S}}
, which quantifies the temporal irreversibility
of a system out of equilibrium
Pruessner and Garcia-Millan 2025
;
Sekimoto 2012
,

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{S}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{w}^{2}}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}+\frac{4}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\left(-\mathsf{D}_{x}W^{\prime\prime}(x)+(W^{\prime}(x))^{2}\right)P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}}}(x)

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{\mathsf{D}_{x}}\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}x\,}\left(-\mathsf{D}_{x}W^{\prime\prime}(x)+(\mathsf{w}-W^{\prime}(x))^{2}\right)P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle+-$}}}(x)\ .

(60)

A free run-and-tumble particle produces entropy at rate
\mathsf{w}^{2}/\mathsf{D}_{x}

Cocconi et al. 2020
. Here, we use
the correlation functions
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle++$}}}
and
P_{\hskip-2.0pt{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle-+$}}}
calculated analytically, Sec.
V.2
,
to characterise how the entropy production varies as the soft repulsion
\bar{\nu}
is
increased.
As shown in Fig.
6
, both an increasing repulsion as well as an increasing interaction length lead
to a decreased rate of entropy production
as particles jam more frequently, thereby effectively slowing down.

VI
Discussion and Conclusion

In this paper we have characterised soft, interacting run-and-tumble particles
in a one-dimensional, periodic domain using a microscopic field theory.
By means of an iterative scheme, we show how the effective interaction vertices are calculated analytically,
characterising the stationary state of the two-particle system.
We further use the effective interaction vertices to calculate a number of observables that quantify
the emergence of effective interaction
Garcia-Millan et al. 2026
.

The effective interaction vertices are calculated in a systematic perturbation expansion in the interaction coupling.
We include finite-size effects by accounting for discrete wavenumbers
k_{j}
in the Fourier convention
and by using Matsubara frequency sums to calculate the loop corrections.
More and more terms are generated in the ensuing iterative scheme. In principle all terms can be tracked,
however, we pragmatically neglect some that we demonstrate are multiplied by an
exponentially small coefficient provided
L\xi^{-1}
,
\gamma L^{2}/\mathsf{D}_{x}\gg 1
.

Our iterative method exploits the simple, somewhat propagator-like
form of the Yukawa potential, which makes the terms more easily tractable at every order.
From the form of the Yukawa interaction potential also stems the fact that the effective interaction vertices and
pair correlation functions are linear combinations of the rescaled potential and its spatial derivative.
Extending our method to a general soft interaction potential and many interacting particles
remains an open question
of great interest.

RG-M was supported in part by the European Research Council under the EU’s Horizon 2020
Programme (Grant number 740269),
and acknowledges support from a St John’s College Research Fellowship, University of Cambridge.
